<#sep#># Gravitationaw wens

A wight source passes behind a gravitationaw wens (point mass pwaced in de center of de image). The aqwa circwe is de wight source as it wouwd be seen if dere was no wens, white spots are de muwtipwe images (or Einstein ring) of de source.

A gravitationaw wens is a distribution of matter (such as a cwuster of gawaxies) between a distant wight source and an observer, dat is capabwe of bending de wight from de source as de wight travews towards de observer. This effect is known as gravitationaw wensing, and de amount of bending is one of de predictions of Awbert Einstein's generaw deory of rewativity.[1][2] (Cwassicaw physics awso predicts de bending of wight, but onwy hawf dat predicted by generaw rewativity.[3])

Awdough Einstein made unpubwished cawcuwations on de subject in 1912,[4] Orest Khvowson (1924)[5] and Frantisek Link (1936)[6] are generawwy credited wif being de first to discuss de effect in print. However, dis effect is more commonwy associated wif Einstein, who pubwished an articwe on de subject in 1936.[7]

Fritz Zwicky posited in 1937 dat de effect couwd awwow gawaxy cwusters to act as gravitationaw wenses. It was not untiw 1979 dat dis effect was confirmed by observation of de so-cawwed "Twin QSO" SBS 0957+561.

## Description

Gravitationaw wensing – intervening gawaxy modifies appearance of a gawaxy far behind it (video; artist's concept).
This schematic image shows how wight from a distant gawaxy is distorted by de gravitationaw effects of a foreground gawaxy, which acts wike a wens and makes de distant source appear distorted, but magnified, forming characteristic rings of wight, known as Einstein rings.
An anawysis of de distortion of SDP.81 caused by dis effect has reveawed star-forming cwumps of matter.

Unwike an opticaw wens, a gravitationaw wens produces a maximum defwection of wight dat passes cwosest to its center, and a minimum defwection of wight dat travews furdest from its center. Conseqwentwy, a gravitationaw wens has no singwe focaw point, but a focaw wine. The term "wens" in de context of gravitationaw wight defwection was first used by O.J. Lodge, who remarked dat it is "not permissibwe to say dat de sowar gravitationaw fiewd acts wike a wens, for it has no focaw wengf".[8] If de (wight) source, de massive wensing object, and de observer wie in a straight wine, de originaw wight source wiww appear as a ring around de massive wensing object. If dere is any misawignment, de observer wiww see an arc segment instead. This phenomenon was first mentioned in 1924 by de St. Petersburg physicist Orest Chwowson,[9] and qwantified by Awbert Einstein in 1936. It is usuawwy referred to in de witerature as an Einstein ring, since Chwowson did not concern himsewf wif de fwux or radius of de ring image. More commonwy, where de wensing mass is compwex (such as a gawaxy group or cwuster) and does not cause a sphericaw distortion of space–time, de source wiww resembwe partiaw arcs scattered around de wens. The observer may den see muwtipwe distorted images of de same source; de number and shape of dese depending upon de rewative positions of de source, wens, and observer, and de shape of de gravitationaw weww of de wensing object.[10]

There are dree cwasses of gravitationaw wensing:[8][11]

1. Strong wensing: where dere are easiwy visibwe distortions such as de formation of Einstein rings, arcs, and muwtipwe images.

2. Weak wensing: where de distortions of background sources are much smawwer and can onwy be detected by anawyzing warge numbers of sources in a statisticaw way to find coherent distortions of onwy a few percent. The wensing shows up statisticawwy as a preferred stretching of de background objects perpendicuwar to de direction to de centre of de wens. By measuring de shapes and orientations of warge numbers of distant gawaxies, deir orientations can be averaged to measure de shear of de wensing fiewd in any region, uh-hah-hah-hah. This, in turn, can be used to reconstruct de mass distribution in de area: in particuwar, de background distribution of dark matter can be reconstructed. Since gawaxies are intrinsicawwy ewwipticaw and de weak gravitationaw wensing signaw is smaww, a very warge number of gawaxies must be used in dese surveys. These weak wensing surveys must carefuwwy avoid a number of important sources of systematic error: de intrinsic shape of gawaxies, de tendency of a camera's point spread function to distort de shape of a gawaxy and de tendency of atmospheric seeing to distort images must be understood and carefuwwy accounted for. The resuwts of dese surveys are important for cosmowogicaw parameter estimation, to better understand and improve upon de Lambda-CDM modew, and to provide a consistency check on oder cosmowogicaw observations. They may awso provide an important future constraint on dark energy.

3. Microwensing: where no distortion in shape can be seen but de amount of wight received from a background object changes in time. The wensing object may be stars in de Miwky Way in one typicaw case, wif de background source being stars in a remote gawaxy, or, in anoder case, an even more distant qwasar. The effect is smaww, such dat (in de case of strong wensing) even a gawaxy wif a mass more dan 100 biwwion times dat of de Sun wiww produce muwtipwe images separated by onwy a few arcseconds. Gawaxy cwusters can produce separations of severaw arcminutes. In bof cases de gawaxies and sources are qwite distant, many hundreds of megaparsecs away from our Gawaxy.

Gravitationaw wenses act eqwawwy on aww kinds of ewectromagnetic radiation, not just visibwe wight. Weak wensing effects are being studied for de cosmic microwave background as weww as gawaxy surveys. Strong wenses have been observed in radio and x-ray regimes as weww. If a strong wens produces muwtipwe images, dere wiww be a rewative time deway between two pads: dat is, in one image de wensed object wiww be observed before de oder image.

## History

One of Eddington's photographs of de 1919 sowar ecwipse experiment, presented in his 1920 paper announcing its success

Henry Cavendish in 1784 (in an unpubwished manuscript) and Johann Georg von Sowdner in 1801 (pubwished in 1804) had pointed out dat Newtonian gravity predicts dat starwight wiww bend around a massive object[12] as had awready been supposed by Isaac Newton in 1704 in his Queries No.1 in his book Opticks.[13] The same vawue as Sowdner's was cawcuwated by Einstein in 1911 based on de eqwivawence principwe awone.[8] However, Einstein noted in 1915, in de process of compweting generaw rewativity, dat his (and dus Sowdner's) 1911-resuwt is onwy hawf of de correct vawue. Einstein became de first to cawcuwate de correct vawue for wight bending.[14]

The first observation of wight defwection was performed by noting de change in position of stars as dey passed near de Sun on de cewestiaw sphere. The observations were performed in 1919 by Ardur Eddington, Frank Watson Dyson, and deir cowwaborators during de totaw sowar ecwipse on May 29.[15] The sowar ecwipse awwowed de stars near de Sun to be observed. Observations were made simuwtaneouswy in de cities of Sobraw, Cear<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

struct Interval {
    int s, e, idx;
};

int main() {
    ios::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(0);

    int n, m;
    cin >> n >> m;

    vector<Interval> intervals;
    for (int i = 0; i < m; ++i) {
        int a, l;
        cin >> a >> l;
        int s = a;
        int e = a + l - 1;
        intervals.push_back({s, e, i + 1});
    }

    sort(intervals.begin(), intervals.end(), [](const Interval& a, const Interval& b) {
        return a.s < b.s;
    });

    vector<Interval> merged;
    for (const auto& it : intervals) {
        if (merged.empty()) {
            merged.push_back(it);
        } else {
            auto& last = merged.back();
            if (it.s <= last.e) {
                if (it.e > last.e) {
                    last.e = it.e;
                }
            } else {
                merged.push_back(it);
            }
        }
    }

    vector<vector<Interval>> groups(merged.size());
    for (const auto& it : intervals) {
        int s = it.s;
        int low = 0, high = merged.size() - 1;
        int j = 0;
        while (low <= high) {
            int mid = (low + high) / 2;
            if (merged[mid].s <= s) {
                j = mid;
                low = mid + 1;
            } else {
                high = mid - 1;
            }
        }
        groups[j].push_back(it);
    }

    vector<int> keep;
    for (int i = 0; i < merged.size(); ++i) {
        auto& g = groups[i];
        if (g.empty()) continue;

        sort(g.begin(), g.end(), [](const Interval& a, const Interval& b) {
            return a.s < b.s;
        });

        vector<int> s_list, e_list;
        for (const auto& it : g) {
            s_list.push_back(it.s);
            e_list.push_back(it.e);
        }

        int n_group = s_list.size();
        vector<int> max_e(n_group), max_idx(n_group);
        max_e[0] = e_list[0];
        max_idx[0] = 0;
        for (int j = 1; j < n_group; ++j) {
            if (e_list[j] > max_e[j - 1]) {
                max_e[j] = e_list[j];
                max_idx[j] = j;
            } else {
                max_e[j] = max_e[j - 1];
                max_idx[j] = max_idx[j - 1];
            }
        }

        int L = merged[i].s;
        int R_merged = merged[i].e;
        int current_end = L;

        while (current_end < R_merged) {
            auto it = upper_bound(s_list.begin(), s_list.end(), current_end);
            int j = it - s_list.begin() - 1;

            if (j < 0) break;

            int best_e = max_e[j];
            int best_idx = max_idx[j];

            if (best_e <= current_end) break;

            keep.push_back(g[best_idx].idx);
            current_end = best_e;
        }
    }

    vector<bool> is_kept(m + 1, false);
    for (int idx : keep) {
        is_kept[idx] = true;
    }

    vector<int> removed;
    for (int i = 1; i <= m; ++i) {
        if (!is_kept[i]) {
            removed.push_back(i);
        }
    }

    cout << removed.size() << '\n';
    for (size_t i = 0; i < removed.size(); ++i) {
        if (i > 0) cout << ' ';
        cout << removed[i];
    }
    cout << '\n';

    return 0;

k=9 mod4 is 1 → 9 mod4=1 → 9^9 mod4. 9 is 1 mod4, so exponent 9 → 1^9 mod4<#sep#>[API] Detector tallies of a single bin are difficult to work with
## Summary of issue
When using a detector w/ a single bin, e.g. one group flux, the bin data in the output file is a single bin. Rather than a proper array, these are floats cast into array form. Indexing doesn't work, but you can get the "values" by converting the arrays to `float`.

## Code for reproducing the issue

[tally_det0.txt](https://github.com/CORE-GATECH-GROUP/serpent-tools/files/2340714/tally_det0.txt)
```
import serpentTools
r = serpentTools.read('tally_det0.txt', 'det')
one = r['one']
one.tallies
# array(8.19E17)
r['two'].tallies
# array([8.19E17, 7.18E17])
r['two'].tallies[0]
# 8.19E17
one.tallies[0]
```
## Actual outcome including console output and error traceback if applicable
This raises an `IndexError`

## Expected outcome
Better way to index tally data of a single point.  Plotting also doesn't work for this as well, as the tally data has no shape, but is of size 1.

Converting these to floats, or an array of a single float, e.g. array([tally]), would help with plotting and slicing down the road.

Labeling as `API-Incompatible` because, depending on the implementation, this could require changes from the user. If this is the case, then this should be included with a release, like 0.6.0

## Versions

* Version  from ``serpentTools.__version__`` `0.5.3`

The first observation of wight defwection was performed by noting de change in position of stars as dey passed near de Sun on de cewestiaw sphere. The observations were performed in 1919 by Ardur Eddington, Frank Watson Dyson, and deir cowwaborators during de totaw sowar ecwipse on May 29.[15] The sowar ecwipse awwowed de stars near de Sun to be observed. Observations were made simuwtaneouswy in de cities of Sobraw, Ceará, Braziw and in São Tomé and Príncipe on de west coast of Africa.[16] The observations demonstrated dat de wight from stars passing cwose to de Sun was swightwy bent, so dat stars appeared swightwy out of position, uh-hah-hah-hah.[17]

Bending wight around a massive object from a distant source. The orange arrows show de apparent position of de background source. The white arrows show de paf of de wight from de true position of de source.
In de formation known as Einstein's Cross, four images of de same distant qwasar appear around a foreground gawaxy due to strong gravitationaw wensing.

The resuwt was considered spectacuwar news and made de front page of most major newspapers. It made Einstein and his deory of generaw rewativity worwd-famous. When asked by his assistant what his reaction wouwd have been if generaw rewativity had not been confirmed by Eddington and Dyson in 1919, Einstein said "Then I wouwd feew sorry for de dear Lord. The deory is correct anyway."[18] In 1912, Einstein had specuwated dat an observer couwd see muwtipwe images of a singwe wight source, if de wight were defwected around a mass. This effect wouwd make de mass act as a kind of gravitationaw wens. However, as he onwy considered de effect of defwection around a singwe star, he seemed to concwude dat de phenomenon was unwikewy to be observed for de foreseeabwe future since de necessary awignments between stars and observer wouwd be highwy improbabwe. Severaw oder physicists specuwated about gravitationaw wensing as weww, but aww reached de same concwusion dat it wouwd be nearwy impossibwe to observe.[7]

Awdough Einstein made unpubwished cawcuwations on de subject,[4] de first discussion of de gravitationaw wens in print was by Khvowson, in a short articwe discussing de “hawo effect” of gravitation when de source, wens, and observer are in near-perfect awignment,[5] now referred to as de Einstein ring.

In 1936, after some urging by Rudi W. Mandw, Einstein rewuctantwy pubwished de short articwe "Lens-Like Action of a Star By de Deviation of Light In de Gravitationaw Fiewd" in de journaw Science.[7]

In 1937, Fritz Zwicky first considered de case where de newwy discovered gawaxies (which were cawwed 'nebuwae' at de time) couwd act as bof source and wens, and dat, because of de mass and sizes invowved, de effect was much more wikewy to be observed.[19]

In 1963 Yu. G. Kwimov, S. Liebes, and Sjur Refsdaw recognized independentwy dat qwasars are an ideaw wight source for de gravitationaw wens effect.[20]

It was not untiw 1979 dat de first gravitationaw wens wouwd be discovered. It became known as de "Twin QSO" since it initiawwy wooked wike two identicaw qwasistewwar objects. (It is officiawwy named SBS 0957+561.) This gravitationaw wens was discovered by Dennis Wawsh, Bob Carsweww, and Ray Weymann using de Kitt Peak Nationaw Observatory 2.1 meter tewescope.[21]

In de 1980s, astronomers reawized dat de combination of CCD imagers and computers wouwd awwow de brightness of miwwions of stars to be measured each night. In a dense fiewd, such as de gawactic center or de Magewwanic cwouds, many microwensing events per year couwd potentiawwy be found. This wed to efforts such as Opticaw Gravitationaw Lensing Experiment, or OGLE, dat have characterized hundreds of such events, incwuding dose of OGLE-2016-BLG-1190Lb and OGLE-2016-BLG-1195Lb.

## Expwanation in terms of space–time curvature

Simuwated gravitationaw wensing (bwack howe passing in front of a background gawaxy).

In generaw rewativity, wight fowwows de curvature of spacetime, hence when wight passes around a massive object, it is bent. This means dat de wight from an object on de oder side wiww be bent towards an observer's eye, just wike an ordinary wens. In Generaw Rewativity de speed of wight depends on de gravitationaw potentiaw (aka de metric) and dis bending can be viewed as a conseqwence of de wight travewing awong a gradient in wight speed. Light rays are de boundary between de future, de spacewike, and de past regions. The gravitationaw attraction can be viewed as de motion of undisturbed objects in a background curved geometry or awternativewy as de response of objects to a force in a fwat geometry. The angwe of defwection is:

${\dispwaystywe \deta ={\frac {4GM}{rc^{2}}}}$

toward de mass M at a distance r from de affected radiation, where G is de universaw constant of gravitation and c is de speed of wight in a vacuum. This formuwa is identicaw to de formuwa for weak gravitationaw wensing derived using rewativistic Newtonian dynamics [22] widout curving spacetime.

Since de Schwarzschiwd radius ${\dispwaystywe r_{\madrm {s} }}$ is defined as ${\dispwaystywe r_{\madrm {s} }={2Gm}/{c^{2}}}$, dis can awso be expressed in simpwe form as

${\dispwaystywe \deta =2{\frac {r_{\madrm {s} }}{r}}}$

This image from de NASA/ESA Hubbwe Space Tewescope shows de gawaxy cwuster MACS J1206.

This has opened a whowe new avenue for research ranging from finding very distant objects to finding vawues for cosmowogicaw parameters so we can understand de universe better.

A simiwar search in de soudern hemisphere wouwd be a very good step towards compwementing de nordern hemisphere search as weww as obtaining oder objectives for study. If such a search is done using weww-cawibrated and weww-parameterized instrument and data, a resuwt simiwar to de nordern survey can be expected. The use of de Austrawia Tewescope 20 GHz (AT20G) Survey data cowwected using de Austrawia Tewescope Compact Array (ATCA) stands to be such a cowwection of data. As de data were cowwected using de same instrument maintaining a very stringent qwawity of data we shouwd expect to obtain good resuwts from de search. The AT20G survey is a bwind survey at 20 GHz freqwency in de radio domain of de ewectromagnetic spectrum. Due to de high freqwency used, de chances of finding gravitationaw wenses increases as de rewative number of compact core objects (e.g. qwasars) are higher (Sadwer et aw. 2006). This is important as de wensing is easier to detect and identify in simpwe objects compared to objects wif compwexity in dem. This search invowves de use of interferometric medods to identify candidates<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

vector<int> compute_k_core(int n, const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int k) {
    vector<int> degree(n + 1);
    for (int u = 1; u <= n; ++u) {
        degree[u] = adj[u].size();
    }
    vector<bool> deleted(n + 1, false);
    queue<int> q;
    for (int u = 1; u <= n; ++u) {
        if (degree[u] < k) {
            q.push(u);
            deleted[u] = true;
        }
    }
    while (!q.empty()) {
        int u = q.front();
        q.pop();
        for (int v : adj[u]) {
            if (!deleted[v]) {
                --degree[v];
                if (degree[v] < k && !deleted[v]) {
                    deleted[v] = true;
                    q.push(v);
                }
            }
        }
    }
    vector<int> core;
    for (int u = 1; u <= n; ++u) {
        if (!deleted[u]) {
            core.push_back(u);
        }
    }
    return core;
}

void solve() {
    int n, m, k;
    cin >> n >> m >> k;
    vector<vector<int>> adj(n + 1);
    for (int i = 0; i < m; ++i) {
        int u, v;
        cin >> u >> v;
        adj[u].push_back(v);
        adj[v].push_back(u);
    }
    vector<int> core = compute_k_core(n, adj, k);
    if (!core.empty()) {
        cout << "1 " << core.size() << '\n';
        for (int u : core) {
            cout << u << ' ';
        }
        cout << '\n';
        return;
    }
    if (k == 1) {
        cout << "1 1\n1\n";
        return;
    }
    if (k == 2) {
        for (int u = 1; u <= n; ++u) {
            if (!adj[u].empty()) {
                cout << "2 2\n" << u << ' ' << adj[u][0] << '\n';
                return;
            }
        }
        cout << "-1\n";
        return;
    }
    int max_degree = 0;
    for (int u = 1; u <= n; ++u) {
        max_degree = max(max_degree, (int)adj[u].size());
    }
    if (max_degree < k - 1) {
        cout << "-1\n";
        return;
    }
    vector<vector<int>> sorted_adj(n + 1);
    for (int u = 1; u <= n; ++u) {
        sorted_adj[u] = adj[u];
        sort(sorted_adj[u].begin(), sorted_adj[u].end());
    }
    for (int u = 1; u <= n; ++u) {
        if ((int)adj[u].size() < k - 1) continue;
        for (int v : adj[u]) {
            auto& a = sorted_adj[u];
            auto& b = sorted_adj[v];
            int i = 0, j = 0;
            vector<int> common;
            while (i < a.size() && j < b.size() && common.size() < k - 2) {
                if (a[i] == v) { i++; continue; }
                if (b[j] == u) { j++; continue; }
                if (a[i] == b[j]) {
                    common.push_back(a[i]);
                    i++;
                    j++;
                } else if (a[i] < b[j]) {
                    i++;
                } else {
                    j++;
                }
            }
            if (common.size() >= k - 2) {
                vector<int> clique = {u, v};
                for (int l = 0; l < k - 2; ++l) {
                    clique.push_back(common[l]);
                }
                cout << "2\n";
                for (int x : clique) {
                    cout << x << ' ';
                }
                cout << '\n';
                return;
            }
        }
    }
    cout << "-1\n";
}

int main() {
    ios::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);
    int t;
    cin >> t;
    while (t--) {
        solve();
    }
    return 0;

</think>Given a right triangle \(ABC\) with \(\widehat{A} = 90^\circ\), \(AB = 5\), and the length of the angle bisector \(IC = 6\), we need to find the length of \(BC\).

1. **Setting up the problem:**
   - Let \(AB = 5\), \(AC = b\), and \(BC = c\).
   - The inradius \(r\) of the right triangle is given by \(r = \frac{AB + AC - BC}{2} = \frac{5 + b - c}{2}\).

2. **Using the distance formula:**
   - The coordinates of the incenter \(I\) are \((r, r)\) and the coordinates of point \(C\) are \((b, 0)\).
   - The distance \(IC\) is given by \(\sqrt{(b - r)^2 + r^2} = 6\).
   - Substituting \(r = \frac{5 + b - c}{2}\) into the distance formula, we get:
     \[
     \sqrt{\left(b - \frac{5 + b - c}{2}\right)^2 + \left(\frac{5 + b - c}{2}\right)^2} = 6
     \]

## Summary of issue
When reading a file with assembly discontinuity factors (ADFs), there is a string that serpentTools attempts to convert to a vector. This causes a ValueError
## Code for reproducing the issue
Add the following code to a section of a result file
```
% Assembly discontinuity factors (order: W-S-E-N / NW-NE-SE-SW):

DF_SURFACE                (idx, [1:  3])  = 'ADF' ;
DF_SYM                    (idx, 1)        = 1 ;
DF_N_SURF                 (idx, 1)        = 4 ;
DF_N_CORN                 (idx, 1)        = 4 ;
```
```
$ python -c "import serpentTools; serpentTools.read(\"adf_res.m\")"
```

## Sowar gravitationaw wens

Awbert Einstein predicted in 1936 dat rays of wight from de same direction dat skirt de edges of de Sun wouwd converge to a focaw point approximatewy 542 AUs from de Sun, uh-hah-hah-hah.[33] Thus, a probe positioned at dis distance (or greater) from de Sun couwd use de Sun as a gravitationaw wens for magnifying distant objects on de opposite side of de Sun, uh-hah-hah-hah.[34] A probe's wocation couwd shift around as needed to sewect different targets rewative to de Sun, uh-hah-hah-hah.

This distance is far beyond de progress and eqwipment capabiwities of space probes such as Voyager 1, and beyond de known pwanets and dwarf pwanets, dough over dousands of years 90377 Sedna wiww move farder away on its highwy ewwipticaw orbit. The high gain for potentiawwy detecting signaws drough dis wens, such as microwaves at de 21-cm hydrogen wine, wed to de suggestion by Frank Drake in de earwy days of SETI dat a probe couwd be sent to dis distance. A muwtipurpose probe SETISAIL and water FOCAL was proposed to de ESA in 1993, but is expected to be a difficuwt task.[35] If a probe does pass 542 AU, magnification capabiwities of de wens wiww continue to act at farder distances, as de rays dat come to a focus at warger distances pass furder away from de distortions of de Sun's corona.[36] A critiqwe of de concept was given by Landis,[37] who discussed issues incwuding interference of de sowar corona, de high magnification of de target, which wiww make de design of de mission focaw pwane difficuwt, and an anawysis of de inherent sphericaw aberration of de wens.

## Measuring weak wensing

Gawaxy cwuster MACS J2129-0741 and wensed gawaxy MACS2129-1.[38]

Kaiser, Sqwires and Broadhurst (1995),[39] Luppino & Kaiser (1997)[40] and Hoekstra et aw. (1998) prescribed a medod to invert de effects of de Point Spread Function (PSF) smearing and shearing, recovering a shear estimator uncontaminated by de systematic distortion of de PSF. This medod (KSB+) is de most widewy used medod in weak wensing shear measurements.[41][42]

Gawaxies have random rotations and incwinations. As a resuwt, de shear effects in weak wensing need to be determined by statisticawwy preferred orientations. The primary source of error in wensing measurement is due to de convowution of de PSF wif de wensed image. The KSB medod measures de ewwipticity of a gawaxy image. The shear is proportionaw to de ewwipticity. The objects in wensed images are parameterized according to deir weighted qwadrupowe moments. For a perfect ewwipse, de weighted qwadrupowe moments are rewated to de weighted ewwipticity. KSB cawcuwate how a weighted ewwipticity measure is rewated to de shear and use de same formawism to remove de effects of de PSF.[43]

KSB's primary advantages are its madematicaw ease and rewativewy simpwe impwementation, uh-hah-hah-hah. However, KSB is based on a key assumption dat de PSF is circuwar wif an anisotropic distortion, uh-hah-hah-hah. This is a reasonabwe assumption for cosmic shear surveys, but de next generation of surveys (e.g. LSST) may need much better accuracy dan KSB can provide.

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

struct Edge {
    int u, v;
    char c;
};

pair<bool, pair<int, vector<int>>> solve(int n, const vector<Edge>& edges, char target) {
    vector<vector<pair<int, int>>> adj(n + 1);
    for (const auto& e : edges) {
        int ci = (e.c != target) ? 1 : 0;
        adj[e.u].emplace_back(e.v, ci);
        adj[e.v].emplace_back(e.u, ci);
    }

    vector<int> color(n + 1, -1);
    vector<int> flips;
    int total = 0;
    bool possible = true;

    for (int u = 1; u <= n; ++u) {
        if (color[u] == -1) {
            queue<int> q;
            q.push(u);
            color[u] = 0;
            vector<int> component = {u};
            int sum = 0;
            bool ok = true;

            while (!q.empty()) {
                int current = q.front();
                q.pop();

                for (const auto& p : adj[current]) {
                    int v = p.first;
                    int ci = p.second;
                    int expected = color[current] ^ ci;

                    if (color[v] == -1) {
                        color[v] = expected;
                        component.push_back(v);
                        sum += expected;
                        q.push(v);
                    } else if (color[v] != expected) {
                        ok = false;
                    }
                }
                if (!ok) break;
            }

            if (!ok) {
                possible = false;
                break;
            }

            int comp_size = component.size();
            int flip = min(sum, comp_size - sum);
            total += flip;
            bool invert = (sum > comp_size - sum);

            for (int x : component) {
                if ((color[x] == 1 && !invert) || (color[x] == 0 && invert)) {
                    flips.push_back(x);
                }
            }
        }
    }

    if (possible) {
        return {true, {total, flips}};
    } else {
        return {false, {0, {}}};
    }
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int n, m;
    cin >> n >> m;
    vector<Edge> edges(m);
    for (int i = 0; i < m; ++i) {
        int u, v;
        char c;
        cin >> u >> v >> c;
        edges[i] = {u, v, c};
    }

    auto [possible_r, res_r] = solve(n, edges, 'R');
    auto [possible_b, res_b] = solve(n, edges, 'B');

    if (!possible_r && !possible_b) {
        cout << -1 << '\n';
        return 0;
    }

    pair<int, vector<int>> best;
    if (!possible_r) {
        best = res_b;
    } else if (!possible_b) {
        best = res_r;
    } else {
        if (res_r.first <= res_b.first) {
            best = res_r;
        } else {
            best = res_b;
        }
    }

    cout << best.first << '\n';
    for (size_t i = 0; i < best.second.size(); ++i) {
        if (i > 0) cout << ' ';
        cout << best.second[i];
    }
    cout << '\n';
    
    return 0;

## Summary of issue

Detectors with [time binning](http://serpent.vtt.fi/mediawiki/index.php/Input_syntax_manual#det_di) have 13 columns in the detector output file (an additional column indicating the time bin between the normal 1st and 2nd column) and thus get mixed up with the Serpent 1 style detector output with the number of scores as the 13th column.

The Detector Reader reads the tally values from column 11 and error values from column 12 as usual. The problem is that in detectors with time binning, the tally values are actually at column 12 and error values at column 13.  

I'm not sure what's the best approach for this at serpentTools side, but the best way to figure out whether the detector that has 13 columns has time binning is to check for the

`DET<name>T`

array that is printed in the detector file and gives the time binning associated with the detector.

## Code for reproducing the issue

Serpent input:

    set title "Homogeneous case"

    % --- Disable group constant calculation

    set gcu -1

    % --- Geometry is just a cube

    surf 2 cuboid -100.0 100.0 -100.0 100.0 -100.0 100.0

    % --- Cell definitions:

    cell  3  0  fuel     -2
    cell 99  0  outside   2     % Outside world

    % --- Fuel material:

    mat fuel   sum vol 8.0000E+06
    8016.03c  0.316667
    1001.03c  0.633333
    92235.03c 0.0015
    92238.03c 0.0485
    5010.03c  0.000115797

    % --- Reflective boundary conditions:

    set bc 2 2 2

    % --- Neutron population:

    set pop 1000 10 10

    % --- Normalization

    set power 1.0

    % --- Specify a time binnig (10 bins between 0 and 50 microseconds)

    tme dettime 2 10 0 50e-6

    % --- Detector with time binning

    det FP dr -8 void di dettime

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;
using ll = long long;

vector<ll> sieve(ll n) {
    vector<bool> is_prime(n+1, true);
    is_prime[0] = is_prime[1] = false;
    for (ll i = 2; i*i <= n; ++i) {
        if (is_prime[i]) {
            for (ll j = i*i; j <= n; j += i)
                is_prime[j] = false;
        }
    }
    vector<ll> res;
    for (ll i = 2; i <= n; ++i)
        if (is_prime[i])
            res.push_back(i);
    return res;
}

ll count_pi(ll n) {
    if (n < 2) return 0;
    ll v = sqrt(n), s = (v + 1) / 2;
    vector<ll> small(s), rough(s), lare(s);
    for (ll i = 0; i < s; ++i) {
        small[i] = i;
        rough[i] = 2*i + 1;
        lare[i] = (n/(2*i+1) -1)/2;
    }
    vector<bool> skip(v+1, false);
    ll pc = 0;
    for (ll p = 3; p <= v; p += 2) {
        if (skip[p]) continue;
        ll q = p*p;
        pc++;
        if (q > v) break;
        for (ll i = q; i <= v; i += 2*p)
            skip[i] = true;
    }
    ll ptr = 0;
    for (ll i = 1; i <= v; i += 2)
        if (!skip[i])
            rough[ptr++] = i;
    rough.resize(ptr);
    lare.resize(ptr);
    for (ll j = 0; j < ptr; ++j) {
        ll p = rough[j];
        if (p*p > n) break;
        ll cnt = 0;
        for (ll i = p; i <= v; i += 2*p)
            if (!skip[i]) cnt++;
        ll ns = 0;
        for (ll k = 0; k < ptr; ++k) {
            ll i = rough[k];
            if (skip[i]) continue;
            ll d = i*p;
            lare[k] -= (d <= v ? lare[(d-1)/2] : small.back()) - cnt;
            rough[ns++] = i;
        }
        ptr = ns;
        for (ll j = (v/p)|1; j >= p; j -= 2) {
            ll c = small[j/2];
            for (ll i = j*p; i <= v; i += 2*p) {
                if (small[i/2] < c) break;
                small[i/2] -= c;
            }
        }
    }
    ll res = lare[0] + ptr + 1;
    for (ll k = 1; k < ptr; ++k)
        res -= lare[k];
    for (ll l = 1; l < ptr; ++l) {
        ll q = rough[l];
        ll m = n / q;
        if (m <= v) break;
        ll cnt = 0;
        for (ll i = 0; i < ptr; ++i) {
            ll p = rough[i];
            if (p > m / q) break;
            cnt += small[(m/p)/2];
        }
        res -= cnt - l;
    }
    return res;
}

int main() {
    ll n;
    cin >> n;

    ll cube_count = 0;
    if (n >= 8) {
        ll cube_root = pow(n, 1.0/3);
        while ((cube_root + 1) * (cube_root + 1) * (cube_root + 1) <= n) cube_root++;
        cube_count = sieve(cube_root).size();
    }

    ll product_count = 0;
    if (n >= 6) {
        ll sqrt_n = sqrt(n);
        auto primes = sieve(sqrt_n);
        vector<ll> precomp = sieve(1e6);
        for (ll p : primes) {
            ll x = n / p;
            if (x <= p) continue;
            ll cnt;
            if (x <= 1e6) {
                auto it_low = upper_bound(precomp.begin(), precomp.end(), p);
                auto it_high = upper_bound(precomp.begin(), precomp.end(), x);
                cnt = it_high - it_low;
            } else {
                cnt = count_pi(x) - count_pi(p);
            }
            product_count += cnt;
        }
    }

    cout << cube_count + product_count << endl;
    return 0;

So the integral becomes integral from u=2 to u=1 of (1/u)(-du) = integral from 1 to 2 of (1/u) du = ln u from 1 to 2 = ln 2 - ln 1 = ln 2. So the integral is ln 2. Therefore, the original limit would be ln 2. So the answer is ln 2, which is approximately 0.693...

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;
using ll = long long;

ll powmod(ll a, ll b, ll m) {
    ll res = 1;
    a %= m;
    while (b) {
        if (b & 1) res = res * a % m;
        a = a * a % m;
        b >>= 1;
    }
    return res;
}

ll inv(ll a, ll m) {
    return powmod(a, m-2, m);
}

ll order(ll a, ll p) {
    ll ord = p - 1;
    ll temp = ord;
    for (ll i = 2; i * i <= temp; ++i) {
        if (temp % i) continue;
        while (ord % i == 0 && powmod(a, ord / i, p) == 1)
            ord /= i;
        while (temp % i == 0)
            temp /= i;
    }
    if (temp > 1) {
        if (powmod(a, ord / temp, p) == 1)
            ord /= temp;
    }
    return ord;
}

ll discrete_log(ll a, ll b, ll m) {
    a %= m;
    b %= m;
    if (a == 0) return b == 0 ? 1 : -1;
    ll k = 1, add = 0, g;
    while ((g = gcd(a, m)) > 1) {
        if (b == k) return add;
        if (b % g) return -1;
        b /= g;
        m /= g;
        add++;
        k = k * (a / g) % m;
    }
    ll n = sqrt(m) + 1;
    unordered_map<ll, ll> vals;
    for (ll q = 0, cur = b; q <= n; ++q) {
        vals[cur] = q;
        cur = cur * a % m;
    }
    ll an = powmod(a, n, m);
    for (ll p = 1, cur = k; p <= n; ++p) {
        cur = cur * an % m;
        if (vals.count(cur))
            return n * p - vals[cur] + add;
    }
    return -1;
}

bool solve(ll k, ll l, ll r, ll p) {
    if (p == 2) {
        if (k % 2 == 0) return 1 % 2 == 1;
        else {
            return (1 % 2 == 1);
        }
    }
    if (k % p == 0) {
        return 1 % p == 1;
    }
    ll d = (p - 1) * inv(k, p) % p;
    ll a = 2 % p;
    ll ord = order(a, p);
    ll m = discrete_log(a, d, p);
    if (m == -1) return false;
    ll min_m = m;
    while (min_m <= r) {
        if (min_m >= l) return true;
        min_m += ord;
    }
    return false;
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(0);
    cout.tie(0);
    int t;
    cin >> t;
    while (t--) {
        ll k, l, r, p;
        cin >> k >> l >> r >> p;
        if (k % p == 0) {
            cout << 1 % p << '\n';
            continue;
        }
        if (p == 2) {
            cout << (1 % 2) << '\n';
            continue;
        }
        ll d = (p - 1) * inv(k % p, p) % p;
        ll a = 2 % p;
        ll ord = order(a, p);
        ll m = discrete_log(a, d, p);
        bool found = false;
        if (m != -1) {
            ll base = m;
            if (base > r) {
                found = false;
            } else {
                ll steps = (r - base) / ord;
                ll minimal = base + steps * ord;
                if (minimal >= l && minimal <= r) {
                    found = true;
                } else {
                    if (base >= l && base <= r)
                        found = true;
                    else {
                        ll candidate = base + ((l - base + ord - 1) / ord) * ord;
                        if (candidate <= r)
                            found = true;
                    }
                }
            }
        }
        if (found) {
            cout << 0 << '\n';
        } else {
            cout << 1 << '\n';
        }
    }

Wait, another idea. Let's note that the sum S(t) can be rewritten as sum_{k=1}^\infty t^k/(1 + t^k) = sum_{k=1}^\infty 1/(1 + t^{-k}) as before. Let me substitute m = k. Not sure. Hmm.

Alternatively, consider that for t approaching 1 from below, let's set t = e^{-ε} where ε approaches 0 from above. Then 1 - t ≈ ε. So the expression becomes (ε) * sum_{k=1}^\infty e^{-ε k}/(1 + e^{-ε k}).

Then, as ε approaches 0, the sum over k can be approximated by an integral. Let’s set x = ε k, so k = x/ε. Then, the sum is approximately (1/ε) * sum_{k=1}^\infty ε e^{-x}/(1 + e^{-x}) ≈ (1/ε) * integral_{x=0}^\infty e^{-x}/(1 + e^{-x}) dx. Then multiplying by ε, we get the integral from 0 to infinity of e^{-x}/(1 + e^{-x}) dx, which we already computed as ln 2.

Therefore, this method suggests the limit is ln 2. Let me verify this with another approach.

Alternatively, consider expanding 1/(1 + t^k) as a geometric series. Wait, since t < 1, t^k < 1, so 1/(1 + t^k) can be written as a sum_{n=0}^\infty (-1)^n t^{k n} }, but this is valid only if t^k < 1, which it is since t < 1. But alternating series might complicate things.

But let's try:

S(t) = sum_{k=1}^\infty t^k/(1 + t^k) = sum_{k=1}^\infty t^k * sum_{n=0}^\infty (-1)^n t^{k n} }.

Interchange the sums: sum_{n=0}^\infty (-1)^n sum_{k=1}^\infty t^{k(n + 1)} }.

Which is sum_{n=0}^\infty (-1)^n [t^{n + 1}/(1 - t^{n + 1})].

Wait, that might not converge. For example, when n=0, term is (-1)^0 [t/(1 - t)] = t/(1 - t), and for n=1, it's (-1)^1 [t^2/(1 - t^2)], etc. So S(t) becomes sum_{n=0}^\infty (-1)^n t^{n + 1}/(1 - t^{n + 1}).

But this seems complicated. However, if we multiply by (1 - t), then (1 - t) S(t) = sum_{n=0}^\infty (-1)^n t^{n + 1} (1 - t)/(1 - t^{n + 1}).

Note that (1 - t)/(1 - t^{n + 1}) = 1/(1 + t + t^2 + ... + t^n).

So (1 - t) S(t) = sum_{n=0}^\infty (-1)^n t^{n + 1}/(1 + t + t^2 + ... + t^n).

But this doesn't seem straightforward to evaluate. Maybe another approach is needed.

Alternatively, if we set t = e^{-ε} as before, and take ε approaching 0, then t^{n + 1} ≈ e^{-ε(n + 1)} ≈ 1 - ε(n + 1). But the denominator 1 + t + ... + t^n = (1 - t^{n + 1})/(1 - t) ≈ (1 - (1 - ε(n + 1)))/ε ≈ (ε(n + 1))/ε = n + 1. Therefore, each term becomes (-1)^n (1 - ε(n + 1)) / (n + 1). Therefore, approximately:

(1 - t) S(t) ≈ sum_{n=0}^\infty (-1)^n (1 - ε(n + 1)) / (n + 1).

But as ε approaches 0, the leading term is sum_{n=0}^\infty (-1)^n / (n + 1) = sum_{m=1}^\infty (-1)^{m - 1}/m = ln 2. Because the Taylor series for ln(1 + x) is sum_{m=1}^\infty (-1)^{m - 1} x^m / m, evaluated at x=1 gives ln 2. Therefore, the leading term is ln 2, and the next term is of order ε, which disappears as ε approaches 0. Hence, the limit is ln 2.

This matches the earlier result from the integral approximation. Therefore, the answer should be ln 2.

Another verification: Maybe compute the sum numerically for t close to 1 and check. Let's take t = 0.99. Then compute (1 - 0.99) * sum_{k=1}^\infty (0.99)^k / (1 + (0.99)^k). Compute the first few terms and see if it approaches ln 2 ≈ 0.6931.

First, 1 - t = 0.01.

Compute sum_{k=1}^\infty (0.99)^k / (1 + (0.99)^k). Let's compute terms until they are negligible.

For k=1: 0.99/(1 + 0.99) ≈ 0.99/1.99 ≈ 0.4975

k=2: 0.99^2 / (1 + 0.99^2) ≈ (0.9801)/(1 + 0.9801) ≈ 0.9801 / 1.9801 ≈ 0.495

k=3: 0.97.../ similar, each term is roughly decreasing.

But since t is close to 1, the terms decay slowly. So the sum up to k=1000 would contribute. But this is tedious by hand. Alternatively, approximate the sum using the integral we had. The sum is approximately (1/ε) ∫_{0}^\infty e^{-x}/(1 + e^{-x}) dx where ε = 0.01. Then (1/ε) * ln 2. Then (1 - t) S(t) ≈ (ε) * (1/ε) * ln 2 = ln 2. So numerical approximation for t=0.99 would give close to ln 2. This gives more confidence.

Therefore, after considering different methods and verifying via integral approximation and series expansion, the conclusion is that the limit is ln 2.

Thus, count(r) is:

if k0 >n → 0 else (n - k0)//m +1.

So, for each residue r, we can compute count(r), which is the number of terms in the second part (k from m+1 to n) that have residue r mod m.

Now the question is, for those count(r) terms for a given r, how to compute their total contribution mod m?

For example, for r=0, then all these terms are multiples of m. Thus their contribution is (0)^ (k) mod m. But since 0^0 is 1? Wait in our case, when k=2m, the terms would be (2m)^ (2m) mod m. 

Wait, 2m mod m =0, so 0^{2m} mod m. But x^y mod m where x is 0 and y>0 will be 0 mod m. So any term where k is divisible by m will have term mod m equal to 0. Because 0 to the any positive exponent is 0 mod m. Thus all terms with residue r=0 mod m contribute 0.

Thus, for residue r=0, the contribution is zero. So count(r0) = count(r=0) can be ignored as they don't contribute.

Now, for other residues r from 1 to m-1:

We must compute the sum over all terms with residue r mod m and k in the second part (from m+1 to n) of k^k mod m.

Each of these terms is (k)^k mod m, but k = a = m*q + r, so:

a mod m = r, exponent is a= m*q + r mod m. The term is (r)^{m*q +r} mod m.

We can split the exponent:

(r)^{a} mod m = (r^r) * (r)^{m*q} mod m.

So for each residue r (not 0), compute the part:

sum_{q such that a= m*q +r is in the terms (k)} [ (r^r * r^{m*q} ) mod m ]

Now, for each residue r not 0:

We can split the exponent here into two parts, but the r and m could have common factors. Let's consider coprimes and non coprimes.

Let’s first handle the case when r and m are coprimes (gcd(r,m)=1):

As we said before, using Euler’s theorem, since r and m are coprimes, r^ϕ(m) ≡1 mod m. 

Thus, r^{m*q} can be written as (r^{ϕ(m)})^{ (m*q / ϕ(m))} ) * r^{ m*q mod ϕ(m) } → which is 1^{something} * r^{m*q modϕ(m)}.

Thus, r^{m*q mod ϕ(m)} mod m.

Thus, the term's exponent for the r^{m*q} part can be taken mod ϕ(m).

Thus, the term becomes:

term = ( r^r * r^{ (m*q mod ϕ) } ) mod m.

Let’s denote phi = phi(m). 

Let’s define exponent_part = m*q mod phi.

Wait but the exponent is m*q, so mod phi(m):

m*q mod phi is (m mod phi) * (q mod (phi / gcd(m, phi))) ) * ?

Not sure. So regardless, the exponent m*q is considered modulo phi(m). 

Therefore, the exponents m*q can be considered modulo phi(m), so the factor r^{m*q} mod m is periodic in q with period T_r, where T_r is the least period of m*q mod phi(m) as q increases. 

The key here is that for varying q, the exponent (m*q) mod phi(m) forms an arithmetic progression in the exponent's terms, with the step being m. 

Therefore, the sequence of exponents (m*q mod phi(M)) mod phi(m) forms a sequence: m*1 mod phi, m*2 mod phi, ..., and this sequence has a period of (phi(M) / gcd(m, phi(M)) )). 

Wait the period of an arithmetic sequence with step 'a' modulo mod is indeed mod / gcd(a, mod). 

This is because the terms are a, 2a, 3a<#sep#>Support for non-integer named universes for branching reader
## Summary of issue

Serpent 2 allows universe names to be quite generic string literals. However, the branching reader of serpentTools only seems to support universe names that are integers.

This seems to be related to the int conversion on line 113 of serpentTools/parsers/branching.py (commit dd886ff):

`unvID, numVariables = [int(xx) for xx in self._advance()]`

Simply limiting the int conversion to numVariables reads the universe in without problems.

## Code for reproducing the issue

```
#!/usr/bin/env python3
# -*- coding: utf-8 -*-

import serpentTools

# --- Try to read in a .coe file with a non-integer universe name

serpentTools.read("./problematic.coe")
```

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

struct Rectangle {
    int x1, y1, x2, y2;
};

struct ColumnSegmentTree {
    int n;
    vector<int> tree;

    ColumnSegmentTree(int size) : n(size) {
        tree.resize(4 * (size + 1), 1e9);
        build(1, 1, size);
    }

    void build(int node, int start, int end) {
        if (start == end) {
            tree[node] = start;
            return;
        }
        int mid = (start + end) / 2;
        build(2 * node, start, mid);
        build(2 * node + 1, mid + 1, end);
        tree[node] = min(tree[2 * node], tree[2 * node + 1]);
    }

    int query(int a, int b, int node, int start, int end) {
        if (b < start || a > end) return 1e9;
        if (a <= start && end <= b) return tree[node];
        int mid = (start + end) / 2;
        int left = query(a, b, 2 * node, start, mid);
        if (left != 1e9) return left;
        return query(a, b, 2 * node + 1, mid + 1, end);
    }

    int find_first_available(int a, int b) {
        return query(a, b, 1, 1, n);
    }

    void update(int pos, int node, int start, int end) {
        if (start == end) {
            tree[node] = 1e9;
            return;
        }
        int mid = (start + end) / 2;
        if (pos <= mid) update(pos, 2 * node, start, mid);
        else update(pos, 2 * node + 1, mid + 1, end);
        tree[node] = min(tree[2 * node], tree[2 * node + 1]);
    }

    void mark_used(int j) {
        update(j, 1, 1, n);
    }
};

int main() {
    ios::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int n, q;
    cin >> n >> q;
    vector<Rectangle> rects(q);
    for (int i = 0; i < q; ++i) {
        cin >> rects[i].x1 >> rects[i].y1 >> rects[i].x2 >> rects[i].y2;
    }

    ColumnSegmentTree col_tree(n);
    int ans = 0;

    for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i) {
        vector<pair<int, int>> forbidden;
        for (const auto& rect : rects) {
            if (rect.x1 <= i && i <= rect.x2) {
                forbidden.emplace_back(rect.y1, rect.y2);
            }
        }
        sort(forbidden.begin(), forbidden.end());

        vector<pair<int, int>> allowed;
        int start = 1;
        for (const auto& [y1, y2] : forbidden) {
            if (y1 > start) {
                allowed.emplace_back(start, y1 - 1);
            }
            start = max(start, y2 + 1);
        }
        if (start <= n) {
            allowed.emplace_back(start, n);
        }

        for (const auto& [a, b] : allowed) {
            int j = col_tree.find_first_available(a, b);
            if (j <= n) {
                ans++;
                col_tree.mark_used(j);
                break;
            }
        }
    }

    cout << ans << '\n';
}<#sep#> where ε = 0.01. Then (1/ε) * ln 2. Then (1 - t) S(t) ≈ (ε) * (1/ε) * ln 2 = ln 2. So numerical approximation for t=0.99 would give close to ln 2. This gives more confidence.

Therefore, after considering different methods and verifying via integral approximation and series expansion, the conclusion is that the limit is ln 2.
</think>To evaluate the limit \(\lim_{t\to1^{-}}(1-t)\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{t^{k}}{1+t^{k}}\), we start by making a substitution to simplify the expression. Let \( t = e^{-\varepsilon} \) where \(\varepsilon \to 0^+\) as \( t \to 1^{-} \). Thus, \(1 - t \approx \varepsilon\).

Rewriting the sum in terms of \(\varepsilon\), we have:
\[
(1 - t) \sum_{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{t^k}{1 + t^k} \approx \varepsilon \sum_{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{e^{-\varepsilon k}}{1 + e^{-\varepsilon k}}
\]

Next, we approximate the sum by an integral. Notice that as \(\varepsilon \to 0\), the sum can be approximated by the integral:
\[
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{e^{-\varepsilon k}}{1 + e^{-\varepsilon k}} \approx \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \int_{0}^{\infty} \frac{e^{-x}}{1 + e^{-x}} \, dx
\]

This approximation is valid because the sum \(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} f(\varepsilon k)\) can be approximated by the integral \(\frac{1}{\varepsilon} \int_{0}^{\infty} f(x) \, dx\) when \(\varepsilon\) is small. Here, \( f(x) = \frac{e^{-x}}{1 + e^{-x}} \).

Thus, the original expression becomes:
\[
\varepsilon \sum_{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{e^{-\varepsilon k}}{1 + e^{-\varepsilon k}} \approx \varepsilon \cdot \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \int_{0}^{\infty} \frac{e^{-x}}{1 + e^{-x}} \, dx = \int_{0}^{\infty} \frac{e^{-x}}{1 + e^{-x}} \, dx
\]

Now, we need to evaluate the integral:
\[
\int_{0}^{\infty} \frac{e^{-x}}{1 + e^{-x}} \, dx
\]

To solve this integral, we make the substitution \( u = e^{-x} \), so \( du = -e^{-x} \, dx \) or \( dx = -\frac{du}{u} \). The limits of integration change from \( x \) from \( 0 \) to \( \infty \) to \( u \) from \( 1 \) to \( 0 \):
\[
\int_{0}^{\infty} \frac{e^{-x}}{1 + e^{-x}} \, dx = \int_{1}^{0} \frac{u}{1 + u} \left( -\frac{du}{u} \right) = \int_{0}^{1} \frac{1}{1 + u} \, du
\]

This integral is straightforward:
\[
\int_{0}^{1} \frac{1}{1 + u} \, du = \left. \ln(1 + u) \right|_{0}^{1} = \ln(2) - \ln(1) = \ln(2)
\]

Therefore, the limit is:
\[
\lim_{t \to 1^{-}} (1 - t) \sum_{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{t^k}{1 + t^k} = \ln(2)
\]

So the final answer is:
\[
\boxed{\ln 2}
\]<#sep#> is that for varying q, the exponent (m*q) mod phi(m) forms an arithmetic progression in the exponent's terms, with the step being m. 

Therefore, the sequence of exponents (m*q mod phi(M)) mod phi(m) forms a sequence: m*1 mod phi, m*2 mod phi, ..., and this sequence has a period of (phi(M) / gcd(m, phi(M)) )). 

Wait the period of an arithmetic sequence with step 'a' modulo mod is indeed mod / gcd(a, mod). 

This is because the terms are a, 2a, 3a mod mod. The next term after reaching mod must repeat every (mod/gcd(a,mod)) steps. 

Thus, the terms for m*q mod phi have period T_r_coprime = phi(m)/ g, where g = gcd(m, phi(m)).

Similarly, since T_r_coprime is the length of the cycle, the exponent portions for the same residue would cycle every T_r_coprime terms.

Therefore, for residue r coprime with m, the terms consisting of r^{m*q} mod m will have a period T_r_coprime for varying q. 

Thus, the total contribution for such residue r's terms can be summed by determining how many full cycles and a remainder of terms there are in those count(r) terms. For each such cycle, the sum of its terms can be stored in a table and multiplied by the number of cycles. The remaining terms are summed directly.

But even with this periodic approach, how do we calculate the sums for residues that don't have coprime with m?

Let's think of the case when gcd(r, m) > 1. 

Say r and m share a common factor d>1. Let d = gcd(r, m).

In that case, we cannot directly apply Euler’s theorem to r and m. 

But perhaps we can factor m into prime factors and use the Chinese_remainder theorem's components. But maybe it's a bit complicated.

Alternatively, we can compute r^{m*q} mod m directly using the method for exponentials when the base and modulus are not coprime:

Let me think of the term r^{m*q} mod m. 

Factor m into prime powers, and use Chinese Remainder Theorem to combine results.

But this could be time consuming as m is up to 2e5. Alternatively, perhaps compute it directly with modular exponentiation for each term's q, but that would take time proportional to count(r) terms which could be up to 1e18 / (2e5) = ~5e12, which is way too big.

Hmm, so this is a problem. So this approach might still be not feasible unless we can find another way.

Wait but for residues where r does not share a factor with m then we can use the coprimes case, but for other residues, perhaps the exponents can be reduced some other way?

Wait, maybe we can compute the exponents in terms of m*q and find a pattern where the exponents modulo another value gives us periodicity. 

Alternatively, note that in such cases (when r is not coprime to m), the exponent m*q may still have some period modulo some number smaller than phi(m). For instance, if m is a prime power?

Alternatively, I'm not sure. This is getting quite complex. Perhaps there is a better way.

Let me look at the problem constraints again. 

The value of m is up to 2e5. Let me think of calculating each term from k=1 up to m and then for k beyond m, note that the exponents mod m repeat every m terms? Or perhaps every some small number of terms?

Wait wait, let's think of the terms when k is greater than or equal to m and look at k mod m.

The terms are based on their residue r =k mod m.

But the exponent is k = m*t +r, so the term is (r)^{m*t +r} mod m.

r^{m*q + r} = r^r * (r^{m})^q.

So the exponent m*q + r can be written as r plus m * q, which is the same as the above. So yes, so term is indeed r^r multiplied by (r^m)^q mod m.

But wait the exponent in question for the multiplier term is exactly q, since (r^m)^q = r^{m * q}.

So given this, term for residue r, q is:

r^r mod m multiplied by (r^m)^q mod m, then mod m.

Thus, for each residue r (excluding r=0 which contributes zero), we have term_r = (r^r mod m) * pow(r^m mod m, q, m) mod m.

Now, pow(r^m mod m, q, m) is (r^m)^q mod m.

Thus, the term_r can be calculated for each q by multiplying the initial power and raising the (r^m) to the q'th power mod m.

Now, for each residue r, the terms form a geometric progression if (r^m mod m) is a constant term. Wait yes!

Because for each residue r, the terms for that residue are:

First term when q starts from q_start to q_end stepwise:

term when q is a certain value:

term_q = (r^r)* ( (r^m)^{q} mod m) mod m.

So the terms for residue r form a geometric sequence with multiplicative factor (r^m mod m) between terms. Because each step increases q by 1, so the exponent increases by m, or in the multiplier, the effect is multiply by (r^m mod m).

Wait let's see:

Suppose for q=0, then the term is r^r mod m. (assuming q starts at zero here).

For q=1: the term is r^r * (r^m mod m)^1 mod m.

For q=2, r^r * (r^m mod m)^2 mod m.

So yes, the terms form a geometric progression where each term is the previous multiplied by (r^m mod m). So this is a geometric sequence with ratio = (r^m mod m).

Thus, the sum over all terms for residue r from q_start to q_end (inclusive) can be computed using the formula for geometric series mod m.

#define _USE_MATH_DEFINES
#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <algorithm>
#include <cmath>
#include <iomanip>

using namespace std;

struct Point {
    double x, y;
    Point(double x_ = 0, double y_ = 0) : x(x_), y(y_) {}
};

double cross(const Point &a, const Point &b) {
    return a.x * b.y - a.y * b.x;
}

double cross(const Point &o, const Point &a, const Point &b) {
    return (a.x - o.x) * (b.y - o.y) - (a.y - o.y) * (b.x - o.x);
}

vector<Point> convex_hull(vector<Point> points) {
    if (points.size() <= 1) return points;
    sort(points.begin(), points.end(), [](const Point &a, const Point &b) {
        return a.x < b.x || (a.x == b.x && a.y < b.y);
    });
    vector<Point> lower, upper;
    for (auto &p : points) {
        while (lower.size() >= 2 && cross(lower[lower.size()-2], lower.back(), p) <= 0) {
            lower.pop_back();
        }
        lower.push_back(p);
    }
    reverse(points.begin(), points.end());
    for (auto &p : points) {
        while (upper.size() >= 2 && cross(upper[upper.size()-2], upper.back(), p) <= 0) {
            upper.pop_back();
        }
        upper.push_back(p);
    }
    lower.pop_back();
    upper.pop_back();
    lower.insert(lower.end(), upper.begin(), upper.end());
    return lower;
}

double area(const vector<Point> &hull) {
    if (hull.size() < 3) return 0;
    double res = 0;
    int n = hull.size();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        const Point &p = hull[i];
        const Point &q = hull[(i+1)%n];
        res += p.x * q.y - q.x * p.y;
    }
    return fabs(res) / 2.0;
}

vector<Point> original_points;

double compute_area(double theta) {
    double c = cos(theta);
    double s = sin(theta);
    vector<Point> rotated;
    for (const auto &p : original_points) {
        rotated.emplace_back(p.x * c - p.y * s, p.x * s + p.y * c);
    }
    auto hull = convex_hull(rotated);
    return area(hull);
}

int main() {
    int n;
    cin >> n;
    original_points.resize(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        double x, y;
        cin >> x >> y;
        original_points[i] = Point(x, y);
    }

    double low = 0.0;
    double high = acos(-1.0);
    const int iterations = 200;
    for (int iter = 0; iter < iterations; ++iter) {
        double mid1 = low + (high - low) / 3;
        double mid2 = high - (high - low) / 3;
        double a1 = compute_area(mid1);
        double a2 = compute_area(mid2);
        if (a1 < a2) {
            high = mid2;
        } else {
            low = mid1;
        }
    }

    double theta = (low + high) / 2;
    cout << fixed << setprecision(3) << theta << endl;

    return 0;

</think>To find the probability that a randomly selected point \( P \) within the square with vertices at \((\pm 2, \pm 2)\) is within one unit of the origin, we need to follow these steps:

1. **Calculate the area of the square:**
   The vertices of the square are \((2, 2)\), \((2, -2)\), \((-2, -2)\), and \((-2, 2)\). The side length of the square is the distance between \(-2\) and \(2\), which is \(4\). Therefore, the area of the square is:
   \[
   \text{Area of the square} = 4 \times 4 = 16
   \]

2. **Calculate the area of the circle:**
   The circle is centered at the origin with a radius of \(1\). The area of a circle is given by the formula \(\pi r^2\), where \(r\) is the radius. Thus, the area of the circle is:
   \[
   \text{Area of the circle} = \pi \times (1)^2 = \pi
   \]

3. **Determine the probability:**
   The probability that a randomly selected point \( P \) is within one unit of the origin is the ratio of the area of the circle to the area of the square. Therefore, the probability is:
   \[
   \text{Probability} = \frac{\text{Area of the circle}}{\text{Area of the square}} = \frac{\pi}{16}
   \]

Thus, the probability that a randomly selected point \( P \) within the square with vertices at \((\pm 2, \pm 2)\) is within one unit of the origin is:
\[
\boxed{\frac{\pi}{16}}

( Because the last term is the maximum k= m* q_end +r <=n → q_end = floor( (n -r)/m ). )

Wait how count(r) is calculated:

Earlier was count(r)=0 or floor( (n -k0)/m ) +1. 

k0 for r !=0 is (m +r).

So count(r)= floor( (n - (m +r))/m ) +1.

Since the first term is q_start=1 (q=1 → k=m+ r).

Now, the terms are:

term(q) = (r^r mod m) * (pow(r^m mod m, q, m)) mod m 

for q from q_start=1 to q_end.

So the sum over this residue is:

sum_{q=qs to qe} [ (r^r mod m) * ( (r^m)^q mod m ) mod m ]

= (r^r mod m) * sum_{q= qs to qe} ( (r^m)^q mod m ) mod m 

Let me denote:

a = r^r mod m,

b = (r^m) mod m,

then each term is a * b^q mod m, so the total is a*(b^{ qs) + b^{qs+1} + ... + b^{qe} } mod m 

This sum is a geometric series from exponent qs to qe, with ratio 'b' and first term a*b^qs:

The formula for the geometric series (if b !=1) is a*(b^{ q_end+1 } - b^{ q_start } )/(b-1).

If b ==1, then the sum is a*(qe - qs +1 ) mod m.

But since we are working modulo m, we can't just divide by (b-1) unless (b-1) is coprime with m. Alternatively, to compute this fraction mod m, we would need to multiply the inverse of (b-1) mod m, which may not exist if b and m share factors. So maybe it's better to compute the sum using the geometric series formula and handle cases mod m directly with loops?

But since the count(r) can be as large as n/m ( ~ 1e18/2e5 = ~5e12 ), it's still too large.

Hence, this approach can only work if the geometric series has a period modulo m, so that the sum over each full period can be computed, and then multiply by the number of periods plus remainder terms.

Thus, the next idea is to find the periodicity of the geometric series terms mod m. 

The terms b^q mod m will form a periodic sequence with period T. Once this T can be determined, then we can compute the sum over one period and then multiply by the number of full periods, plus the remaining terms' contribution.

So, for each residue r, the multiplicative ratio is b = (r^m mod m). Let’s find the period of the sequence s_q = b^q mod m. The period is the smallest T>0 such that for all q, s_{q+T} = s_q mod m.

The least period T of b mod m is given by:

T is the multiplicative order of B modulo m, where B = b = (r^m mod m). 

But only if gcd(B, m) =1? Not sure. The multiplicative order is typically defined when B and m are coprimes. But even if they are not coprimes, we can find a period by considering the exponent's effect mod m. 

Alternatively, when the ratio b has a period mod m for its powers:

The sequence b, b^2 mod m, b^3 mod m ... will eventually repeat its values. So we can compute the sequence until a repeat is found, store the period T and the sum over a period.

However for large m up to 2e5, doing this for each residue (up to 2e5) is feasible, because even 2e5 * say 1e6 steps each would be too big. So it's necessary to find an efficient way to compute the period T for each residue's b-value.

Alternatively, can the period T be bounded?

Note that for each residue r, the ratio b= (r^m mod m). So for that:

The maximal possible period T for residue r is at most m, but maybe smaller. Or perhaps máx period could be up to O(phi(m)), but not sure.

Wait, the maximum possible period T for b mod m can be the multiplicative order of B modulo m (if coprime), or similar. 

But multiplicative order requires B and m coprime. 

When B and m are not coprime, the sequence terms could still form a periodic pattern but would be increasing multiples until B's exponent has factors with m.

Perhaps in this case, the terms will cycle but only after certain steps.

Hmm, this is getting complex, and perhaps it's more straightforward to try to code this approach with the following steps:

1. Precompute Phi(m) for possible m (but perhaps on the fly).

Wait each test case has its own m, so for each test case, compute phi(m) once.

2. Precompute for each r from modulus m (1 to m-1):

  a. Compute the b = (r)^m mod m.

3. For each residue r:

   if b ==0 then the terms with exponent q: b^q mod m will be zero for any exponent. Since b=0, so each term is a*b^q mod m = a*0=0 mod m. So the sum for that residue would be zero if any of the terms exist. 

So if b is zero, then all terms are zero. Thus, the sum contribution from that residue is zero. 

Thus residues with b=0 can be handled as such.

Otherwise (b !=0):

   We need to compute the sum of a multiplied by (b^q) from q=1 to q_end.

Wait for terms starting at q = qs=1 up to qe = q_end.

Let’s see:

The sum is a * ( (b^{qe+1} - b^{ qs }) / (b -1) ) mod m (if b !=1).

Wait, but since division mod m requires inverses, this is tricky. 

Alternatively, let's represent the terms as:

sum_{q= q_start}^{q_end} b^q 

Which is a geometric series between exponents q_start to q_end:

The sum is (b)^q_start + ... + (b)^{q_end} 

The formula is S = b^(q_start) * (b^{ (qe - qs+1)} -1 ) / (b-1) )

Provided b !=1.

Thus, the total contribution is a * S mod m.

Thus, if we can compute this sum S, then multiplying by a mod m gives the total contribution for residue r.

To compute S mod m, we need to compute:

num = (b^(qe - qs +1) -1 )

denominator = (b-1)

S = [b^{qs} * num/denominator] mod m.

Hence, S mod m = ( (pow<#sep#>", 10.0, 2)
+    ('0", 0, 0)
+    ('10", 0, 0)
+    ('0", 10.0, 2)
+    ('30", 0, 0)
+    ('40", 10.0, 2)
+    ('40", 1.0, 1)
+    ('30", 10.0, 2)

#include <vector>
#include <algorithm>
#include <iostream>

using namespace std;

typedef long long ll;

const int MOD = 1e9 + 7;

struct Matrix {
    vector<vector<ll>> mat;
    Matrix(int n) : mat(n, vector<ll>(n, 0)) {}
};

Matrix multiply(const Matrix& a, const Matrix& b) {
    int n = a.mat.size();
    Matrix res(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        for (int j = 0; j < n; ++j) {
            for (int k = 0; k < n; ++k) {
                res.mat[i][j] = (res.mat[i][j] + a.mat[i][k] * b.mat[k][j]) % MOD;
            }
        }
    }
    return res;
}

Matrix power(Matrix a, ll p) {
    int n = a.mat.size();
    Matrix res(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) res.mat[i][i] = 1;
    while (p > 0) {
        if (p & 1) res = multiply(res, a);
        a = multiply(a, a);
        p >>= 1;
    }
    return res;
}

vector<ll> multiply(const Matrix& mat, const vector<ll>& vec) {
    int n = vec.size();
    vector<ll> res(n, 0);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        for (int k = 0; k < n; ++k) {
            res[i] = (res[i] + mat.mat[i][k] * vec[k]) % MOD;
        }
    }
    return res;
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int n, x;
    cin >> n >> x;
    vector<int> d(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        cin >> d[i];
    }

    int m = *max_element(d.begin(), d.end());
    if (m == 0) {
        cout << 1 << endl;
        return 0;
    }

    vector<int> cnt(m + 1, 0);
    for (int di : d) {
        cnt[di]++;
    }

    vector<ll> dp(m, 0);
    dp[0] = 1;
    for (int s = 1; s < m; ++s) {
        for (int k = 1; k <= s; ++k) {
            dp[s] = (dp[s] + cnt[k] * dp[s - k]) % MOD;
        }
    }

    ll sum_initial = 0;
    for (int s = 0; s < m; ++s) {
        sum_initial = (sum_initial + dp[s]) % MOD;
    }

    if (x < m) {
        ll ans = 0;
        for (int s = 0; s <= x; ++s) {
            ans = (ans + dp[s]) % MOD;
        }
        cout << ans << endl;
        return 0;
    }

    int steps = x - m + 1;
    int size = m + 1;
    Matrix trans(size);

    for (int k = 1; k <= m; ++k) {
        trans.mat[0][k - 1] = cnt[k];
    }
    for (int i = 1; i < m; ++i) {
        trans.mat[i][i - 1] = 1;
    }
    trans.mat[m][0] = 1;
    trans.mat[m][m] = 1;

    Matrix trans_pow = power(trans, steps);

    vector<ll> vec(size, 0);
    for (int i = 0; i < m; ++i) {
        vec[i] = dp[m - 1 - i];
    }
    vec[m] = sum_initial;

    vector<ll> res = multiply(trans_pow, vec);
    cout << res[m] % MOD << endl;

    return 0;

Wait<#sep#> = b^(q_start) * (b^{ (qe - qs+1)} -1 ) / (b-1) )

Provided b !=1.

Thus, the total contribution is a * S mod m.

Thus, if we can compute this sum S, then multiplying by a mod m gives the total contribution for residue r.

To compute S mod m, we need to compute:

num = (b^(qe - qs +1) -1 )

denominator = (b-1)

S = [b^{qs} * num/denominator] mod m.

Hence, S mod m = ( (pow(b, qe - qs +1, denominator * something)) -1 ) / (b-1), but this requires division modulo m, which would require the denominator to have an modular inverse modulo m, which is not guaranteed.

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

struct Edge {
    int u, v, w, idx;
    bool operator<(const Edge& other) const {
        return w < other.w;
    }
};

struct DSU {
    vector<int> parent, rank;
    DSU(int n) : parent(n+1), rank(n+1, 1) {
        iota(parent.begin(), parent.end(), 0);
    }
    int find(int u) {
        return parent[u] == u ? u : parent[u] = find(parent[u]);
    }
    bool unite(int u, int v) {
        u = find(u), v = find(v);
        if (u == v) return false;
        if (rank[u] < rank[v]) swap(u, v);
        parent[v] = u;
        rank[u] += rank[v];
        return true;
    }
};

vector<vector<pair<int, int>>> adj;
vector<int> parent, depth;
vector<vector<int>> up;
vector<vector<int>> max_edge;
const int LOG = 20;

void dfs(int u, int p, int w, int d) {
    parent[u] = p;
    depth[u] = d;
    up[0][u] = p;
    max_edge[0][u] = w;
    for (auto [v, edge_w] : adj[u]) {
        if (v != p) {
            dfs(v, p, edge_w, d+1);
        }
    }
}

void build_binary_lifting(int n) {
    up.resize(LOG, vector<int>(n+1));
    max_edge.resize(LOG, vector<int>(n+1));
    dfs(1, 0, 0, 0);
    for (int k = 1; k < LOG; k++) {
        for (int u = 1; u <= n; u++) {
            up[k][u] = up[k-1][up[k-1][u]];
            max_edge[k][u] = max(max_edge[k-1][u], max_edge[k-1][up[k-1][u]]);
        }
    }
}

int get_max_edge(int u, int v) {
    if (u == v) return 0;
    if (depth[u] < depth[v]) swap(u, v);
    int max_w = 0;
    for (int k = LOG-1; k >= 0; k--) {
        if (depth[u] - (1 << k) >= depth[v]) {
            max_w = max(max_w, max_edge[k][u]);
            u = up[k][u];
        }
    }
    if (u == v) return max_w;
    for (int k = LOG-1; k >= 0; k--) {
        if (up[k][u] != up[k][v]) {
            max_w = max({max_w, max_edge[k][u], max_edge[k][v]});
            u = up[k][u];
            v = up[k][v];
        }
    }
    return max({max_w, max_edge[0][u], max_edge[0][v]});
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int n, m;
    cin >> n >> m;
    vector<Edge> edges(m);
    for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) {
        cin >> edges[i].u >> edges[i].v >> edges[i].w;
        edges[i].idx = i;
    }

    sort(edges.begin(), edges.end());
    DSU dsu(n);
    adj.resize(n+1);
    vector<bool> in_mst(m, false);
    for (const Edge& e : edges) {
        if (dsu.unite(e.u, e.v)) {
            in_mst[e.idx] = true;
            adj[e.u].emplace_back(e.v, e.w);
            adj[e.v].emplace_back(e.u, e.w);
        }
    }

    parent.resize(n+1);
    depth.resize(n+1);
    build_binary_lifting(n);

    vector<int> max_weight(m);
    for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) {
        int u = edges[i].u, v = edges[i].v;
        max_weight[i] = get_max_edge(u, v);
    }

    int q;
    cin >> q;
    while (q--) {
        int k;
        cin >> k;
        vector<int> query(k);
        for (int& x : query) {
            cin >> x;
            x--;
        }

        bool possible = true;
        for (int idx : query) {
            if (max_weight[idx] != edges[idx].w) {
                possible = false;
                break;
            }
        }
        if (!possible) {
            cout << "NO\n";
            continue;
        }

        vector<Edge> sorted_query;
        for (int idx : query) {
            sorted_query.push_back(edges[idx]);
        }
        sort(sorted_query.begin(), sorted_query.end());

        DSU q_dsu(n);
        bool has_cycle = false;
        for (int i = 0; i < sorted_query.size();) {
            int current_w = sorted_query[i].w;
            int j = i;
            while (j < sorted_query.size() && sorted_query[j].w == current_w) {
                j++;
            }
            for (int k = i; k < j; k++) {
                int u = sorted_query[k].u;
                int v = sorted_query[k].v;
                if (q_dsu.find(u) == q_dsu.find(v)) {
                    has_cycle = true;
                    break;
                }
                q_dsu.unite(u, v);
            }
            if (has_cycle) break;
            i = j;
        }

        cout << (has_cycle ? "NO" : "YES") << '\n';
    }

    return 0;

Now, let's compute (p - q + r)^3.

First, expand (p - q + r)^3:

= p³ - q³ + r³ + 3(p²(-q) + p² r + (-q)^2 p + (-q)^2 r + r² p + r²(-q)) + 6 p (-q) r.

p³ = (1/9), (-q)³ = - (2/9), r³ = (4/9). So sum is 1/9 - 2/9 + 4/9 = (1 - 2 + 4)/9 = 3/9 = 1/3.

Then the next terms:

3[ p²(-q) + p² r + (-q)^2 p + (-q)^2 r + r² p + r²(-q) ]

Suppose that a, b, c are fractions with denominator 9. For example, possible numerators could be 1, 2, 4, etc., as those are powers of 2, which might relate to the ∛2 term.

Let me try the earlier thought: ∛(1/9) - ∛(2/9) + ∛(4/9). Let's compute this sum.

First, ∛(1/9) is equal to 1/∛9, ∛(2/9) is ∛2 / ∛9, and ∛(4/9) is ∛4 / ∛9. So factoring out 1/∛9, the sum becomes:

(1 - ∛2 + ∛4)/∛9.

Note that ∛4 is (∛2)^2, and ∛9 is 9^(1/3) = (3^2)^(1/3) = 3^(2/3). So 1/∛9 = 3^(-2/3).

So the sum is (1 - ∛2 + (∛2)^2) * 3^(-2/3).

Wait, let's compute (1 - ∛2 + (∛2)^2). Let me compute that expression:

Let u = ∛2. Then the numerator is 1 - u + u². Let's compute 1 - u + u². Notice that 1 + u³ = 1 + 2 = 3. Also, 1 - u + u² is equal to (u³ + 1)/(u + 1). Since u³ = 2, then (u³ + 1) = 3, so (u³ + 1)/(u + 1) = 3/(u + 1). Therefore, 1 - u + u² = 3/(u + 1).

Therefore, the sum becomes 3/(u + 1) * 3^(-2/3) = 3^{1 - 2/3}/(u + 1) = 3^(1/3)/(u + 1).

But u = ∛2, so this is 3^(1/3)/(∛2 + 1). Hmm, does this relate to the original expression?

The original expression is ∛(∛2 - 1) = t.

So if we have t = 3^(1/3)/(∛2 + 1), let's check if this is equal.

# Introduction

This lecture focuses on introducing a set of tools associated with logistic regression.

Today we’re going to use a pretty basic dataset. There are reasons for this (we’ll see this later). This data is from the Institute of Digital Research and Eduation at UCLA. This lecture is a combination of lots of other people’s work and texts.

grad_admit <- read_csv("https://stats.idre.ucla.edu/stat/data/binary.csv") %>%
rank = factor(rank))

The data (explorable below) contains a set of features associated with ‘students’ records for students that applied to UCLA, with a binary variable (admit) which indicates where or not the student was admitted.

library(DT)

It’s standard (and good practice) to explore the data. Below is provided some 1d and 2d visualizations. I would encourage the reader the look into the data and think about the variables we have, how they are distributed and how they relate to each other.

### 1D EDA

Quick look at the distributions (using a function basically wrote on Friday) - feel free to look at it later.

# Create grid of 1d visuals of variable distributions in data frame
# Args:
#   data: data frame you'd like to explore
#   ncol: number of columns for the visual output
#
# Returns:
#   list of graphics
#   and creates the correct visualization
gg1d <- function(data, ncol = 3){
require(gridExtra)
require(ggplot2)
columns <- names(data)
types <- sapply(data, class)
num_types <- c("numeric", "integer")

gglist <- list()

for (i in 1:length(columns)) {
if (types[i] %in% num_types) {
gglist[[i]] <- ggplot(data, aes_string(x = columns[i])) +
geom_histogram()
}else{
gglist[[i]] <- ggplot(data, aes_string(x = columns[i])) +
geom_bar()
}
}
grid.arrange(grobs = gglist, ncol = ncol, top = "1d EDA")
return(gglist)
}
# saving it as "." is a way to ignore the returned list (it still is saved,
#   but "out of sight, out of mind")
. <- gg1d(grad_admit, ncol = 2)

# Logistic Regression

## Quick training/ test split

Before we proceed we will split the data into 2 groups so we can use the second group in assessing model preformance.

train_idx <- sample(1:nrow(grad_admit), size = .5 * nrow(grad_admit))

ga_test  <- grad_admit %>% filter(!(1:nrow(grad_admit) %in% train_idx))

Here’s our logistic regression model (we comment on the structure below).

logit_fit <- glm(admit ~ gre + gpa + rank, data = ga_train,
family = "binomial")
summary(logit_fit)
